Low regularity well-posedness for two-dimensional deep gravity water waves with constant vorticity
Lizhe Wan

Abstract
. We consider the two-dimensional gravity water waves with nonzero constant vorticity in infinite depth. We show that for s ≥ 3 4 s\geq\frac{3}{4} , the water waves system is locally well-posed in ℋ s \mathcal{H}^{s} , which is the nonzero constant vorticity counterpart of the breakthrough work of Ai-Ifrim-Tataru in [ 4 ] . It is also a 1 4 \frac{1}{4} improvement in Sobolev regularity compared to the previous result of Ifrim-Tataru in [ 17 ] .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
76B15, 35Q31

1.
Introduction

We consider gravity water waves with nonzero constant vorticity but without surface tension in two space dimensions.
As shown in Figure
1.1
, the fluid occupies a time-dependent domain
\Omega(t)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
with infinite depth and a free upper boundary
\Gamma(t)
which is asymptotically approaching
y=0
.
Denoting the fluid velocity by
\mathbf{u}(t,x,y)=(u(t,x,y),v(t,x,y))
, the pressure by
p(t,x,y)
, and the constant vorticity by
\gamma
, the equations inside
\Omega(t)
are given by the incompressible Euler equations:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}u_{t}+uu_{x}+vu_{y}=-p_{x}&\\
v_{t}+uv_{x}+vv_{y}=-p_{y}-g&\\
u_{x}+v_{y}=0&\\
\omega=u_{y}-v_{x}=-\gamma.\end{array}\right.

On the boundary
\Gamma(t)
we have the dynamic boundary condition

p=0,

and the kinematic boundary condition

\partial_{t}+\mathbf{u}\cdot\nabla\text{ is tangent to }\Gamma(t).

Here
g>0
is the gravitational constant.

y
x
\Omega(t)
\Gamma(t)

Figure 1.1.
The fluid domain.

1.1.
Water waves in holomorphic coordinates

Let
\mathbf{P}
be the projection onto negative frequencies, with the definition

\mathbf{P}:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I}-iH),

where
H
denotes the Hilbert transform.
We define holomorphic functions on
\mathbb{R}
to be the functions whose Fourier transforms are supported on
(-\infty,0]
;
equivalently in the language of complex analysis, they admit a bounded holomorphic extension onto the lower half-space.
This can be further described by the relation
{\mathbf{P}}f=f
.
Similarly, we define
\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}
to be the projection onto positive frequencies:

\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}:=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{I}+iH)=\mathbf{I}-{\mathbf{P}}.

Functions such that
\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}f=f
are called anti-holomorphic functions.
Anti-holomorphic functions are complex conjugates of holomorphic functions.

As done in the work of Ifrim-Tataru
[
17
]
and Ifrim-Rowan-Tataru-Wan
[
15
]
, we rely here on the holomorphic coordinates and use holomorphic position/velocity potential variables
(W(t,\alpha),Q(t,\alpha))
to express the water waves system.
It is formulated in the following system of equations:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}W_{t}+(W_{\alpha}+1)\underline{F}+i\dfrac{\gamma}{2}W=0&\\
Q_{t}-igW+\underline{F}Q_{\alpha}+i\gamma Q+\mathbf{P}\left[\dfrac{|Q_{\alpha}|^{2}}{J}\right]-i\dfrac{\gamma}{2}T_{1}=0,&\end{array}\right.

where
J:=|1+W_{\alpha}|^{2}
is the Jacobian, and

\displaystyle F:=\mathbf{P}\left[\frac{Q_{\alpha}-\bar{Q}_{\alpha}}{J}\right],\quad

\displaystyle F_{1}=\mathbf{P}\left[\frac{W}{1+\bar{W}_{\alpha}}+\frac{\bar{W}}{1+W_{\alpha}}\right],

\displaystyle\underline{F}:=F-i\frac{\gamma}{2}F_{1},\quad

\displaystyle T_{1}:=\mathbf{P}\left[\frac{W\bar{Q}_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{W}_{\alpha}}-\frac{\bar{W}Q_{\alpha}}{1+W_{\alpha}}\right].

The system (
1.1
) has a conserved energy

(1.2)

\mathcal{E}(W,Q)=\Re\int g|W|^{2}(1+W_{\alpha})-iQ\bar{Q}_{\alpha}+\gamma Q_{\alpha}(\Im W)^{2}-\frac{\gamma^{3}}{2i}|W|^{2}(1+W_{\alpha})\,d\alpha,

and also a conserved horizontal momentum

\mathcal{P}(W,Q)=\int\left\{\frac{1}{i}(\bar{Q}W_{\alpha}-Q\bar{W}_{\alpha})-\gamma|W|^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}(W^{2}\bar{W}_{\alpha}-\bar{W}^{2}W_{\alpha})\right\}d\alpha.

In the case of zero vorticity, the system (
1.1
) is reduced to the irrotational gravity water waves system which was previously studied in the same formulation as
[
13
,
16
,
4
,
3
,
12
]
.
Here for simplicity, we assume the constant vorticity
\gamma>0
.
In the case of the negative vorticity, one can always switch the sign of the variable
\alpha\rightarrow-\alpha
, so that the vorticity can be made positive after the change.

The system (
1.1
) does not have a complete scaling.
The space-time scaling

(1.3)

(W(t,\alpha),Q(t,\alpha))\rightarrow(\lambda^{-2}W(\lambda t,\lambda^{2}\alpha),\lambda^{-3}Q(\lambda t,\lambda^{2}\alpha)),

leaves the gravity
g
unchanged, but it changes the voricity
\gamma
to
\lambda\gamma
.
One the other hand, the pure space scaling

(W(t,\alpha),Q(t,\alpha))\rightarrow(\lambda^{-2}W(t,\lambda^{2}\alpha),\lambda^{-3}Q(t,\lambda^{2}\alpha)),

does not change the constant vorticity
\gamma
, but the gravitational constant
g
becomes
\lambda^{-1}g
.

A simplified model of (
1.1
) is its linearization around the zero solution

(1.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}w_{t}+q_{\alpha}=0&\\
q_{t}+i\gamma q-igw=0,&\end{array}\right.

restricted to holomorphic functions.
(
1.4
) is a linear dispersive equation that can be written as

w_{tt}+i\gamma w_{t}+igw_{\alpha}=0.

Its dispersion relation is given by

\tau^{2}+\gamma\tau+g\xi=0,\quad\xi\leq 0,

whose graph is the intersection of a lateral parabola with the left half plane.
The two intersection points are
(0,0)
and
(0,-\gamma)
.

The conserved energy of (
1.4
) is given by

(1.5)

\mathcal{E}_{0}(w,q)=\int g|w|^{2}-iq\bar{q}_{\alpha}\,d\alpha=g\|w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|q\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}}^{2}.

This conserved energy suggests the functional framework to study (
1.1
).
The system (
1.4
) is well-posed in
\mathcal{H}:=L^{2}\times\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}
space.
To measure the higher regularity of the solution we will use the spaces
\mathcal{H}^{s}
endowed with the norm

\|(w,q)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{s}}:=\|\langle D\rangle^{s}(w,q)\|^{2}_{L^{2}\times\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

We also define the corresponding homogeneous spaces
\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}
given by

\|(w,q)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}}:=\||D|^{s}(w,q)\|^{2}_{L^{2}\times\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}},\quad s\in\mathbb{R}.

The system (
1.1
) is fully nonlinear.
By differentiation, it can be converted into a quasilinear system.
As in the Hunter-Ifrim-Tataru
[
13
]
, we set

{\mathbf{W}}:=W_{\alpha},\quad R:=\frac{Q_{\alpha}}{1+W_{\alpha}},\quad Y:=\frac{W_{\alpha}}{1+W_{\alpha}}.

The function
R
has an intrinsic meaning as it represents the complex velocity on the water surface.
We also need two other auxiliary functions.
The first one
{\underline{a}}
is the
frequency-shift
, and it is given by

{\underline{a}}:=a+\frac{\gamma}{2}a_{1},\quad a:=i(\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[\bar{R}R_{\alpha}]-{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{R}_{\alpha}]),\quad a_{1}:=R+\bar{R}-N,

where the leading part
a
is also called
Taylor coefficient
, and

N:={\mathbf{P}}[W\bar{R}_{\alpha}-\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}R]+\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[\bar{W}R_{\alpha}-{\mathbf{W}}\bar{R}].

The other auxiliary function we need is the
advection velocity

{\underline{b}}
.
Its leading part
b
is also called the
transport coefficient
.

{\underline{b}}:=b-i\frac{\gamma}{2}b_{1},\quad b:={\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{Q_{\alpha}}{J}\right]+\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}\left[\frac{\bar{Q}_{\alpha}}{J}\right],\quad b_{1}:={\mathbf{P}}\left[\frac{W}{1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}\right]-\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}\left[\frac{\bar{W}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}\right].

The expression
g+{\underline{a}}
represents the normal derivative of the pressure on the free boundary, and plays an essential role in the proof.

Using the notation of
{\underline{a}}
and
{\underline{b}}
, one can rewrite (
1.1
) as

(1.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}W_{t}+\underline{b}(W_{\alpha}+1)+i\frac{\gamma}{2}W=\bar{R}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}\bar{W}&\\
Q_{t}+\underline{b}Q_{\alpha}-igW+i\gamma Q-i\frac{\gamma}{2}\bar{R}W=\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[|R|^{2}]-i\frac{\gamma}{2}\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[W\bar{R}-\bar{W}R].&\end{array}\right.

Instead of studying (
1.1
) directly, in this article, we will mostly consider the following
differentiated system.
The pair
({\mathbf{W}},R)
is a good variable because it diagonalizes the differentiated system:

(1.7)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\mathbf{W}_{t}+\underline{b}\mathbf{W}_{\alpha}+\dfrac{(1+\mathbf{W})R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{\mathbf{W}}}=(1+\mathbf{W})\underline{M}+i\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\mathbf{W}(\mathbf{W}-\bar{\mathbf{W}})&\\
R_{t}+\underline{b}R_{\alpha}+i\gamma R-i\dfrac{g\mathbf{W}-a}{1+\mathbf{W}}=i\dfrac{\gamma}{2}\dfrac{R\mathbf{W}+\bar{R}\mathbf{W}+N}{1+\mathbf{W}}.&\end{array}\right.

Here the auxiliary functions are given by

\displaystyle M:=\frac{R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}+\frac{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}-b_{\alpha}=\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[\bar{R}Y_{\alpha}-R_{\alpha}\bar{Y}]+{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}_{\alpha}-\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y],

\displaystyle M_{1}:={\mathbf{W}}-\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}-b_{1,\alpha}={\mathbf{P}}[W\bar{Y}]_{\alpha}-\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[\bar{W}Y]_{\alpha},\qquad\underline{M}:=M-i\frac{\gamma}{2}M_{1}.

Note that
{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}
satisfies the relation

(1.8)

{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}=\frac{R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}+\frac{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}-i\frac{\gamma}{2}({\mathbf{W}}-\bar{{\mathbf{W}}})-\underline{M}.

Using the variable
(Y,R)
, we can rewrite the equation (
1.7
) using the material derivative
D_{t}:=\partial_{t}+{\underline{b}}\partial_{\alpha}
as

(1.9)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}D_{t}Y+|1-Y|^{2}R_{\alpha}=(1-Y)\underline{M}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}[Y^{2}+\frac{\bar{Y}}{1-\bar{Y}}Y(1-Y)]&\\
D_{t}R-i(g+{\underline{a}})Y=-i{\underline{a}}-i\frac{\gamma}{2}(R-\bar{R}).&\end{array}\right.

Again the system (
1.7
) does not have a complete scaling.
However, we can still introduce the
order
of multilinear expressions for (
1.7
) following the work in
[
17
]
.
For single terms, we assign the following orders

•

The order of
|D|^{s}W
is
s-1
.

•

The order of
|D|^{s}R
is
s-\frac{1}{2}
.

•

The order of
\gamma
is
\frac{1}{2}
.

For a multilinear form involving products of such terms, the total order is defined as the sum of the orders of all factors.

Before stating the main low regularity result in this article, we first briefly recall some recent results about this problem and give a short overview of the local well-posedness result in
[
17
]
.
To state them we need to recall and define a series of pointwise and
BMO
control norms

\displaystyle A:=\|\mathbf{W}\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|Y\|_{L^{\infty}}+\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}R\|_{L^{\infty}\cap B^{0}_{\infty,2}},

\displaystyle B:=\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{W}\|_{BMO}+\|R_{\alpha}\|_{BMO},

\displaystyle A_{-\frac{1}{2}}:=\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}W\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|R\|_{L^{\infty}},\quad A_{-1}:=\|W\|_{L^{\infty}}+\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}Q\|_{BMO},

\displaystyle A_{-\frac{3}{2}}:=\||D|^{-\frac{1}{2}}W\|_{L^{\infty}},\quad A_{-2}:=\||D|^{-1}W\|_{L^{\infty}},

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{2}}=\underline{B}:=B+\gamma A+\gamma^{2}A_{-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\underline{A}:=A+\gamma A_{-\frac{1}{2}}+\gamma^{2}A_{-1}+\gamma^{3}A_{-\frac{3}{2}}+\gamma^{4}A_{-2}.

These control norms are defined and used in the energy estimate in
[
17
]
as well as in later works; see for instance Theorem
1.2
in
[
15
]
.
Here the
A_{-1}
norm is slightly different from the one defined in
[
17
]
, where we add an additional control norm
\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}Q\|_{BMO}
.
This new norm helps us get a more refined cubic energy estimate, and does not produce a long energy estimate as Theorem
1.2
.

We also add here additional comments describing these norms.
The control norms
A
and
{\underline{A}}
are invariant with respect to the scaling (
1.3
).
The norm
A_{s}
has
s
more derivatives compared to
A
.
One should remark that although each term of the underline control norms
{\underline{A}}_{s}
does not have the same amount of derivatives, they have the same order.
The difference of orders between
{\underline{A}}_{s}
and
{\underline{A}}
is also
s
.
The underline control norms
{\underline{A}}_{s}
become
A_{s}
in the zero vorticity case.

To complete the description of these norms one should recall that
B
allows for the propagation of regularity of the solutions; the same is true about
{\underline{B}}
which ultimately determines the well-posedness of the system in
[
17
]
.
In this article, we also define the following control norms

\displaystyle A_{\frac{1}{4}}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{1}{4}}_{\infty,2}}+\|R\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{3}{4}}_{\infty,2}},\qquad A_{-\frac{1}{4}}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{B}^{-\frac{1}{4}}_{\infty,2}}+\|R\|_{\dot{B}^{\frac{1}{4}}_{\infty,2}},

\displaystyle A_{-\frac{3}{4}}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{B}^{-\frac{3}{4}}_{\infty,2}}+\|R\|_{\dot{B}_{\infty,2}^{-\frac{1}{4}}},\quad{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}:=A_{\frac{1}{4}}+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}A+\gamma A_{-\frac{1}{4}}+\gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}A_{-\frac{1}{2}}+\gamma^{2}A_{-\frac{3}{4}}.

Here
{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}
can be seen as an intermediate control norm interpolating between
{\underline{A}}
and
{\underline{B}}
.
In our energy estimate below, we no longer use the control norm
{\underline{B}}
, the product
{\underline{A}}{\underline{B}}
is replaced by
{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}
.
For the
L^{2}
and
L^{4}
based control norms, we define

\displaystyle\mathbf{N}_{s}:=\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}\times\dot{H}^{s+\frac{1}{2}}},\quad A^{\sharp}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}+\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{3}{4},4}},\quad A^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}:=\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{2},4}}+\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{1,4}},

\displaystyle{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}:=A^{\sharp}+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{2},4}}+\gamma\|W\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{3}{4},4}}+\gamma\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}+\gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}\|R\|_{L^{4}}+\gamma^{2}\|W\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}+\gamma^{2}\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{-\frac{1}{4},4}},

\displaystyle{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}:=A^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{3}{4},4}}+\gamma\|W\|_{\dot{W}^{1,4}}+\gamma\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{2},4}}+\gamma^{\frac{3}{2}}\|R\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}+\gamma^{2}\|W\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{2},4}}+\gamma^{2}\|R\|_{L^{4}}.

By the Sobolev embedding
\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow BMO(\mathbb{R})
, the control norm
{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}
can be seen roughly as a slightly larger control norm than
{\underline{A}}
.
We also have the following observation for the control norm
{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}
:
{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}\lesssim{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}
.

We remark that in Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
4
]
for the zero vorticity case, the control norms
A,A_{\frac{1}{4}}
and
A^{\sharp}
are used for the energy estimate.
In the case of nonzero vorticity, our control norms need to include the vorticity terms with the same order.
This leads to the above choice of control norms.

1.2.
Some previous results

The constant vorticity water waves system is a special case of free boundary incompressible Euler equations with general vorticity.
Historically a lot of work has been done for the non-constant vorticity case.
For completeness reasons we include some references though the list is by no means exhaustive.
We refer the reader to the following more contemporary references and the references within: Christodoulou-Lindblad
[
9
]
, Coutand-Shkoller
[
19
]
, Lannes
[
21
]
, Lindblad
[
10
]
, Shatah-Zeng
[
25
]
, Zhang-Zhang
[
30
]
.
It is worth mentioning that most of these works rely on a Lagrangian formulation rather than an Eulerian approach in a compact domain.
This is also the case with the most recent work pursued for these model problems, for example, the article of Wang-Zhang-Zhao-Zheng
[
27
]
.
However, the work of Ifrim-Pineau-Tataru-Taylor
[
14
]
departs from this Lagrangian setting and introduces a full Eulerian approach which allows them to obtain sharp local well-posedness for the free boundary Euler equations.

If one imposes conditions on the vorticity, then the free boundary Euler equations become the water wave equations.
There are a lot of existence and well-posedness results on the zero vorticity case.
These results go back to the early works on Nalimov
[
22
]
, Dyachenko
et.al

[
11
,
29
]
and so on.
More relevant to our presentation are the references within the last 20 years and so.
The first local well-posedness result for 2D gravity water wave equations is due to Wu.
Her result on the well-posedness is established in high regularity Sobolev spaces; see
[
28
]
.
Alazard, Burq and Zuily use the tools of paradifferential operators and Strichartz estimates in
[
5
,
6
]
and effectively lower the regularity required for the initial data.
Substantial regularity improvements of these results were obtained by Ai in
[
1
,
2
]
.
In getting these results, Ai relied on a very careful analysis which leads to a novel parametrix construction that gives lossless Strichartz estimates.
Recently, Ai-Ifrim-Tataru devised a new method, called
the balanced energy estimate
in
[
4
]
.
As a direct consequence of this new method, they have the state-of-art result in this realm of problems proving the local well-posedness of 2D gravity water waves with only
\frac{1}{4}
more derivatives above the scaling.
As a side note, it should be mentioned that this method and consequently the regularity improvement on the initial data obtained in
[
4
]
do not rely on the dispersive character of the problem.

The study of constant vorticity water waves is less common. Ifrim-Tataru in
[
17
]
proved the first local well-posedness for large data, as well as cubic lifespan bounds
for small data solutions in a low regularity setting.
Around the same time
Bieri-Miao-Shahshahani-Wu in
[
8
]
proved the local existence of 2D free boundary self-gravitating incompressible fluids with constant vorticity for smooth initial data in bounded domains.
Recently, it was shown in
[
7
]
by Berti-Maspero-Murgante the almost global in time existence result of small amplitude solutions of the 2D gravity-capillary water wave equations with constant vorticity in
\mathbb{T}
.

The use of conformal formulation for two-dimensional water waves originates in early work on traveling waves of Levi-Civita
[
20
]
.
It has been widely used since then in order to study a variety of water wave problems, especially the irrotational pure gravity model such as in
[
13
,
4
,
16
,
3
,
12
]
.
In this article, we follow the formulation and keep mostly the same notations as in the work
[
17
]
of Ifrim and Tataru, though we will introduce new elements/notations.
The authors of
[
17
]
not only establish the cubic energy estimate but also prove that (
1.7
) is locally well-posed in
\mathcal{H}^{1}
.
The author, together with Ifrim, Rowan and Tataru worked further with this formulation in
[
15
]
to show that the constant vorticity gravity water waves can be approximated by the Benjamin-Ono equation and established a better cubic energy estimate.
The author and Rowan also use the holomorphic coordinates in
[
23
,
24
]
to prove the existence of 2D deep gravity and gravity-capillary solitary waves with constant vorticity.

Before going to the main results in this article, we recall some important results in the work of Ifrim-Tataru
[
17
]
and Ifrim-Rowan-Tataru-Wan
[
15
]
, which are considered the basis of our results in this article.
The first result is the main local well-posedness result in
[
17
]
.

Theorem 1.1

(
[
17
]
)
.

Let
n\geq 1
. The system
(
1.1
)
is locally well-posed in
\mathcal{H}^{n}
for initial data
(W_{0},Q_{0})
with the following regularity:

(W_{0},Q_{0})\in\mathcal{H},\quad(\mathbf{W}_{0},R_{0})\in\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{1},

and satisfying the pointwise constraints

\displaystyle|\mathbf{W}(\alpha)+1|>\delta>0

\displaystyle\quad\text{(no interface singularities)},

\displaystyle g+\underline{a}(\alpha)>\delta>0

\displaystyle\quad\text{(Taylor sign condition)}.

Furthermore, the solution can be continued for as long as
{\underline{A}}
,
{\underline{B}}
remain bounded and the pointwise conditions above hold uniformly.
The same result holds in the periodic setting.

Next, it was proved in
[
15
]
the following energy estimates for small initial data.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
15
]
)
.

For any
n\geq 0
there exists an energy functional
E^{n,(3)}
which has the following properties as long as
\underline{A}\ll 1
:

(i)

Norm equivalence:

E^{n,(3)}(\mathbf{W},R)=(1+O(\underline{A}))\mathcal{E}_{0}(\partial^{n}\mathbf{W},\partial^{n}R)+O(\gamma^{4}{\underline{A}}^{2})\mathcal{E}_{0}(\partial^{n-1}\mathbf{W},\partial^{n-1}R).

(ii)

Cubic energy estimates:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{n,(3)}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}}}

\displaystyle\left(\gamma^{2}AB+\gamma^{3}A^{2}+\gamma^{3}BA_{-1/2}+\gamma^{4}AA_{-1/2}+\gamma^{4}A_{-1}B+\gamma^{5}AA_{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\cdot(E^{n,(3)}E^{n-1,(3)})^{\frac{1}{2}}+{\underline{A}}{\underline{B}}E^{n,(3)}.

Here if
n=0
then
\mathcal{E}_{0}(\partial^{-1}\mathbf{W},\partial^{-1}R)
is naturally replaced by
\mathcal{E}(W,Q)
.

\mathcal{E}_{0}
is defined in (
1.5
), and
\mathcal{E}
is the conserved energy (
1.2
).

We also recall that in
[
17
]
the normal form corrections which eliminate the quadratic parts of source terms in (
1.1
):

(1.10)

\displaystyle W^{[2]}=

\displaystyle-(W+\bar{W})W_{\alpha}-\frac{\gamma}{2g}[(Q+\bar{Q})W_{\alpha}+(W+\bar{W})Q_{\alpha}]

\displaystyle+i\frac{\gamma^{2}}{g}\left[(\partial^{-1}W-\partial^{-1}\bar{W})W_{\alpha}+W^{2}+\frac{1}{2}|W|^{2}\right]-\frac{\gamma^{2}}{4g^{2}}(Q+\bar{Q})Q_{\alpha}

\displaystyle+i\frac{i\gamma^{3}}{4g^{2}}[(Q+\bar{Q})W+(\partial^{-1}W-\partial^{-1}\bar{W})Q_{\alpha}]+\frac{\gamma^{4}}{4g^{2}}(\partial^{-1}W-\partial^{-1}\bar{W})W,

\displaystyle Q^{[2]}=

\displaystyle-(W+\bar{W})Q_{\alpha}-\frac{\gamma}{2g}(Q+\bar{Q})Q_{\alpha}+i\frac{\gamma}{4}(W^{2}+2|W|^{2})

\displaystyle+i\frac{\gamma^{2}}{2g}\left[(\partial^{-1}W-\partial^{-1}\bar{W})Q_{\alpha}+\frac{1}{2}(Q+\bar{Q})W\right]+\frac{\gamma^{3}}{4g}(\partial^{-1}W-\partial^{-1}\bar{W})W.

In other words, the new variables
(\tilde{W},\tilde{Q}):=(W+{\mathbf{P}}W^{[2]},Q+{\mathbf{P}}Q^{[2]})
solve the system

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}\tilde{W}_{t}+\tilde{Q}_{\alpha}=G^{\geq 2}(W,Q)&\\
\tilde{Q}_{t}-ig\tilde{W}+i\gamma\tilde{Q}=K^{\geq 2}(W,Q),&\end{array}\right.

where the right-hand side only contains the cubic and higher order nonlinearity of (
1.1
).

We remark that (
1.10
) is the normal form transformation for the original water wave system (
1.1
), but not for the differentiated system (
1.7
).
In order to obtain a low regularity energy estimate for the system (
1.7
), we need to build new paradifferential normal forms that are similar but different from (
1.10
).
Inspired by the above normal forms, the authors in
[
17
]
construct the modified cubic energy that enables them to establish the local well-posedness result.
Later in Section
4
, we will also make use of the above normal form transformation, but rather at the paradifferential level, to construct the paradifferential corrections for the linearized system.

1.3.
The main results

Our balanced energy estimate is the following:

Theorem 1.3
.

For any
s\geq 0
there exists an energy functional
E_{s}
which has the following properties as long as
\max\{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}},{\underline{A}}\}\ll 1
:

(i)

Norm equivalence:

(1.11)

E_{s}(\mathbf{W},R)=(1+O(\underline{A}))\|(\mathbf{W},R)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}}+O(\gamma^{4}\underline{A}^{2})\|(\mathbf{W},R)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s-1}}.

(ii)

Balanced energy estimates:

(1.12)

\frac{d}{dt}E_{s}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{4}}+{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}})E_{s}.

Compared to the previous energy estimates Theorem
1.2
, our energy estimates get improved in the following way:

(1)

We take advantage of the paradifferential structure of the water waves system.
Our energy estimates no longer depend on the control norm
{\underline{B}}
, they now only depend on the control norm
{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}
, which has
\frac{1}{4}
less derivatives compared to
{\underline{B}}
.
This effectively lowers the regularity for well-posedness.
Our normal form corrections are also at the paradifferential level in the same manner as done in the breakthrough work of Ai-Ifrim-Tataru
[
4
]
.

(2)

Compared to the cubic energy estimates in Theorem
1.2
, the right-hand side of our energy estimates no longer depends on
E_{s-1}
.
As a result, we only need to apply Gronwall’s inequality for once and don’t need to use the induction as in
[
17
]
.

(3)

We allow the index
s
to be non-integer, which is more flexible compared to the previous one.

Having established the energy estimate for the full equations, we combine it with the energy estimate for the linearized equations Theorem
4.2
to get the following low regularity well-posedness result.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
s\geq s_{0}=\frac{3}{4}
. The system (
1.7
) is locally well-posed for initial data
({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
in
\mathcal{H}^{s}(\mathbb{R})
(or
\mathbb{T}
) with the following regularity:

({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})\in\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{3}{4}},\qquad\gamma^{2}({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})\in\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{-\frac{1}{4}},

and such that
\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{0}={\underline{A}^{\sharp}}({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
,
{\underline{A}}_{0}={\underline{A}}({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
are small.
Furthermore, the solution can be continued for as long as
\max\{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}},{\underline{A}}\}
remains bounded and
{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{4}}+{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}})\in L^{1}_{t}
.

Although for the second initial condition one can just write
({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})\in\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{3}{4}}\cap\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{-\frac{1}{4}}
, we keep the coefficient
\gamma^{2}
here for book-keeping, and also at time
t=0
, the normal form energy
E_{\frac{3}{4}}({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
constructed in (
1.11
) is bounded.
The control norms at time zero
\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{0}
and
{\underline{A}}_{0}
need to be chosen small so that
O(\underline{A})
and
O(\gamma^{4}\underline{A})
in (
1.11
) are sufficiently small compared to
1
and the implicit constant that depends on
\underline{A}^{\sharp}
in (
1.12
) is bounded at time
t=0
.

Remark 1
.

For local well-posedness, we mean that for all initial data
({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
satisfying the above conditions, there exists a positive time
T>0
depending only on the size of initial data, such that the following holds:

(1)

Unique existence for the regular solutions
: If the initial data is in
\mathcal{H}^{s}
that satisfies the above conditions for some integer
s\geq 1
, then there exist a unique solution
({\mathbf{W}},R)\in\mathcal{H}^{s}
in
[0,T]
, with the property that

\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{C[0,T;\mathcal{H}^{s^{{}^{\prime}}}]}\lesssim\|({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})\|_{C[0,T;\mathcal{H}^{s^{{}^{\prime}}}]},\quad 1\leq s^{{}^{\prime}}\leq s.

(2)

Existence for rough solutions
: For initial data in
\mathcal{H}^{\frac{3}{4}}
, the solution is in
C[0,T;\mathcal{H}^{\frac{3}{4}}]
, and it can be treated as the unique strong limit of smooth solutions.

(3)

Continuous dependence on the data for rough solutions
: If a sequence
({\mathbf{W}}_{j},R_{j})(0)
that converges to
({\mathbf{W}}_{0},R_{0})
in
\mathcal{H}^{\frac{3}{4}}
topology, then
({\mathbf{W}}_{j},R_{j})(t)
also converges to
({\mathbf{W}},R)(t)
in
\mathcal{H}^{\frac{3}{4}}
, for
t\in[0,T]
.

Remark 2
.

In
[
17
]
, in order to prove the local well-posedness, the initial data needs to satisfy the no interface singularities condition:
|{\mathbf{W}}(\alpha)+1|>\delta>0
, and the Taylor sign condition:
g+{\underline{a}}(\alpha)>0
.
Later in (
3.2
), we will prove that
\|{\underline{a}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim{\underline{A}}(1+{\underline{A}})
, so that these two conditions are automatically satisfied when
{\underline{A}}
is small in our setting.

Compared to the previous well-posedness result Theorem
1.1
, our result lowers
\frac{1}{4}
Sobolev regularity.
For simplicity, we will focus on the case of the real line.
For the periodic setting, we refer the reader to the discussion in Appendix
A
of
[
13
]
for the minor changes.

1.4.
The structure of the article

In this article, we use plenty of estimates in paradifferential calculus.
The full system and the linearized system will be rewritten in terms of paradifferential equations.
To get ready for this, we review in Section
2
the definitions and notations of paraproducts and some of the classical Coifman-Meyer paraproduct estimates.
We also introduce the necessary definitions of norms and function spaces we will be using.

Then in Section
3
we apply the paraproduct estimates to auxiliary functions for both Sobolev and
BMO
bounds.
We consider the corresponding bounds for frequency-shift
{\underline{a}}
, advection velocity
{\underline{b}}
and auxiliary functions
Y
and
\underline{M}
.
In addition, we compute the leading terms of para-material derivatives of
{\mathbf{W}},R,W,Y,X:=T_{1-Y}W,Z:=T_{1-Y}Q,
and
U:=T_{1-Y}\partial_{\alpha}^{-1}W
.

Having established the necessary bounds, we consider in Section
4
the linearized system and its energy estimate in
\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}
.
In order to apply the paradifferential calculus, we first rewrite the linearized system as a system of paradifferential equations.
Then we consider the estimates for both linear paradifferential flow and the source terms.
The detailed ideas will be described within this section.

Next, we turn our attention to the energy estimate for the full system in Section
5
.
Again we first reduce the full system to another system of paradifferential equations.
In the following, we show that the paradifferential equations can be further reduced to the linearized paradifferential equations with unbalanced source terms.
In the end, we use paradifferential corrections to eliminate these unfavourable source terms.

Finally, collecting the energy estimates for both linearized and the full systems, we give an outline of proof for the local well-posedness of the water wave system in Section
6
.

Acknowledgments.
The author would like to thank Mihaela Ifrim and Albert Ai for introduction and discussion of many important details in their work
[
4
]
.

2.
Definition of norms and estimates

In this section we review the definition of norms and estimates we will use later in the article.
Our analysis primarily relies on the paradifferential calculus, especially the paraproduct type estimates.

2.1.
Norms and function spaces

We begin with Littlewood-Paley frequency decomposition

I=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}P_{k},

where
P_{k}
are smooth symbols with frequency localized at
2^{k}
.
Most of our analysis is at the level of homogeneous Sobolev spaces
\dot{H}^{s}
, whose norms are given by

\|f\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\sim\|(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|2^{ks}P_{k}f|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}=\|2^{ks}P_{k}f\|_{L^{2}_{\alpha}l^{2}_{k}}.

We recall the Littlewood-Paley square function and its restricted version

S(f)(\alpha):=\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}|P_{k}f(\alpha)|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad S_{>k}(f)(\alpha):=\left(\sum_{j>k}|P_{j}f(\alpha)|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The
BMO
space can be defined using the following square function characterization:

\|f\|_{BMO}^{2}:=\sup_{k}\sup_{|Q|=2^{-k}}2^{k}\int_{Q}|S_{>k}(f)|^{2}\,d\alpha.

For real number
s
we can define the homogeneous spaces
BMO^{s}
with norm

\|f\|_{BMO^{s}}:=\||D|^{s}f\|_{BMO}.

We will also need the following homogeneous Besov spaces
\dot{B}^{s}_{\infty,2}
, whose norms are defined by

\|f\|_{\dot{B}^{s}_{\infty,2}}\sim\left(\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}2^{2sk}\|P_{k}f\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

We have the embedding property
\dot{B}^{s}_{\infty,2}\hookrightarrow BMO^{s}
.

2.2.
Paraproduct and Moser type estimates

For the product of two functions
fg
, we use the Littlewood-Paley paraproduct type decomposition to decompose it as

fg=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{<k-4}g_{k}+\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}f_{k}g_{<k-4}+\sum_{|k-l|<4}f_{k}g_{l}:=T_{f}g+T_{g}f+\Pi(f,g).

For the paraproducts, we have the Hölder type estimates

\displaystyle\|T_{f}g\|_{L^{r}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}},\quad\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q},\quad 1<p\leq\infty,\quad 1<q,r<\infty,

\displaystyle\|\Pi(f,g)\|_{L^{r}}\leq\|f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{L^{q}},\quad\frac{1}{r}=\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q},\quad 1<p,q,r<\infty.

In the case of
q=\infty
, the right-hand side is replaced by
BMO
norms:

(2.1)

\|T_{f}g\|_{L^{p}}+\|\Pi(f,g)\|_{L^{p}}\leq\||D|^{-s}f\|_{L^{p}}\|g\|_{BMO^{s}},\quad 1<p<\infty,s\geq 0.

For the estimate of
T_{f}g
term we have the weaker bound

(2.2)

\|T_{f}g\|_{L^{p}}\leq\|g\|_{\dot{W}^{s,p}}\|f\|_{BMO^{-s}},\quad 1<p<\infty,s>0.

We also have the following commutator estimates:

Lemma 2.1

(
[
4
]
)
.

The following commutator estimates hold for
1<p<\infty
:

\displaystyle\||D|^{s}[{\mathbf{P}},g]|D|^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\||D|^{s+\sigma}g\|_{BMO}\|f\|_{L^{p}},\qquad\sigma\geq 0,\quad s\geq 0,

\displaystyle\||D|^{s}[{\mathbf{P}},g]|D|^{\sigma}f\|_{L^{p}}\lesssim\||D|^{s+\sigma}g\|_{L^{p}}\|f\|_{BMO},\qquad\sigma>0,\quad s\geq 0.

Later these commutator estimates will be applied to functions which are either holomorphic or anti-holomorphic.

Next we consider some product type estimates involving
BMO
or
L^{\infty}
norms.

Lemma 2.2

(
[
4
]
)
.

(1)

The following estimates hold:

(2.3)

\displaystyle\|\Pi(u,v)\|_{BMO}\lesssim\|u\|_{BMO}\|v\|_{BMO},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|P_{\leq k}\Pi_{\geq k}(u,v)\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|u\|_{BMO}\|v\|_{BMO},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\|T_{u}v\|_{BMO}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{BMO},

(2.6)

\displaystyle\|T_{u}v\|_{BMO}\lesssim\|u\|_{BMO^{-s}}\|v\|_{BMO^{s}},\qquad s>0.

(2)

For
s>0
, the space
L^{\infty}\cap BMO^{s}
is an algebra and satisfies the estimate

(2.7)

\|uv\|_{BMO^{s}}\lesssim\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{BMO^{s}}+\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}\|u\|_{BMO^{s}}.

(3)

Furthermore, for any smooth function
F
that vanishes at
0
, then the following Moser type estimates hold:

(2.8)

\|F(u)\|_{BMO^{s}}\lesssim_{\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}}\|u\|_{BMO^{s}}.

As for the estimates in
\dot{H}^{s}
, we have that

Lemma 2.3

(
[
4
]
)
.

Let
s>0
, then
\dot{H}^{s}\cap L^{\infty}
is an algebra, with the estimate

(2.9)

\|uv\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\|v\|_{L^{\infty}}+\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}\|v\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}.

Furthermore, for any smooth function
F
that vanishes at
0
, then the following Moser type estimates hold:

(2.10)

\|F(u)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{\|u\|_{L^{\infty}}}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}.

Below we record the following para-commutators, para-products, and para-associativity lemmas.

Lemma 2.4

(
[
4
]
)
.

(1)

(Para-commutators)
Assume that
s_{1},s_{2}<1
, then we have that

(2.11)

\|T_{f}T_{g}-T_{g}T_{f}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}\rightarrow\dot{H}^{s+s_{1}+s_{2}}}\lesssim\||D|^{s_{1}}f\|_{BMO}\||D|^{s_{2}}g\|_{BMO}.

(2)

(Para-products)
Assume that
s_{1},s_{2}<1
, and
s_{1}+s_{2}\geq 0
, then

(2.12)

\displaystyle\|T_{f}T_{g}-T_{fg}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}\rightarrow\dot{H}^{s+s_{1}+s_{2}}}\lesssim\||D|^{s_{1}}f\|_{BMO}\||D|^{s_{2}}g\|_{BMO},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\|T_{f}T_{g}-T_{fg}\|_{\dot{W}^{s,4}\rightarrow\dot{W}^{s+s_{1}+s_{2},4}}\lesssim\||D|^{s_{1}}f\|_{BMO}\||D|^{s_{2}}g\|_{BMO}.

(3)

(Para-associativity) For
s+s_{2}\geq 0
,
s+s_{1}+s_{2}\geq 0
, and
s_{1}<1
, we have

(2.14)

\displaystyle\|T_{f}\Pi(v,u)-\Pi(v,T_{f}u)\|_{\dot{H}^{s+s_{1}+s_{2}}}\lesssim\||D|^{s_{1}}f\|_{BMO}\||D|^{s_{2}}v\|_{BMO}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{s}},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\|T_{f}\Pi(v,u)-\Pi(v,T_{f}u)\|_{\dot{W}^{s+s_{1}+s_{2},4}}\lesssim\||D|^{s_{1}}f\|_{BMO}\||D|^{s_{2}}v\|_{BMO}\|u\|_{\dot{W}^{s,4}}.

(4)

For
s_{1},s_{2}<1
,
s+s_{1}+s_{2}\geq 0
,
s+s_{2}\geq 0
and
\bar{v}=\bar{P}\bar{v}
, we have

(2.16)

\|T_{f}P(\bar{v}u)-P(\bar{v}T_{f}u)\|_{\dot{H}^{s+s_{1}+s_{2}}}\lesssim\||D|^{s_{1}}f\|_{BMO}\||D|^{s_{2}}v\|_{BMO}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}.

Finally, we record here the para-Leibniz rule.
Define the
para-material derivative
to be

T_{D_{t}}:=\partial_{t}+T_{{\underline{b}}}\partial_{\alpha}.

We then consider the following four versions of para-Leibniz errors.
The first two are unbalanced para-Leibniz errors

\displaystyle E^{p}_{L}(u,v)=T_{D_{t}}T_{u}v-T_{T_{D_{t}}u}v-T_{u}T_{D_{t}}v,

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{p}_{L}(u,v)=T_{D_{t}}T_{u}v-T_{D_{t}u}v-T_{u}T_{D_{t}}v.

The other two are the balanced para-Leibniz errors

\displaystyle E^{\pi}_{L}(u,v)

\displaystyle=T_{D_{t}}\Pi(u,v)-\Pi(T_{D_{t}}u,v)-\Pi(u,T_{D_{t}}v),

\displaystyle\tilde{E}^{\pi}_{L}(u,v)

\displaystyle=T_{D_{t}}\Pi(u,v)-\Pi(D_{t}u,v)-\Pi(u,T_{D_{t}}v).

With above notations, the Leibniz error can be bounded according to the following lemma.

Lemma 2.5

(
[
4
]
)
.

(1)

For the unbalanced para-Leibniz error
E^{p}_{L}(u,v)
we have the bounds

(2.17)

\displaystyle\|E^{p}_{L}(u,v)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{BMO^{\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}}\|v\|_{\dot{H}^{s+\sigma}},\quad\sigma>0,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\|E^{p}_{L}(u,v)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}}\|v\|_{BMO^{s+\sigma}},\quad\sigma>0.

In the case
\sigma=0
the same bounds (
2.17
) and (
2.18
) hold for
\tilde{E}^{p}_{L}(u,v)
with
\sigma=0
.

(2)

For the balanced para-Leibniz error
E^{\pi}_{L}(u,v)
we have the estimate

(2.19)

\|E^{\pi}_{L}(u,v)\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}\|u\|_{BMO^{\frac{1}{4}-\sigma}}\|v\|_{\dot{H}^{s+\sigma}},\qquad\sigma\in\mathbb{R},\quad s\geq 0.

In the case
\sigma=0
, we also have the same bound for
\tilde{E}^{\pi}_{L}(u,v)
.

The proof of this lemma is almost identical to Lemma
3.6
in
[
4
]
, we ask the interested reader to check the proof there.
The only difference is that we replace the bound for
b
by the corresponding estimate for
{\underline{b}}
.

3.
Water waves related bounds

In this section, we first consider the Sobolev and
BMO
bounds of auxiliary functions
Y
,
{\underline{a}}
,
{\underline{b}}
, and
\underline{M}
, then we compute the leading terms of para-material derivatives of
{\mathbf{W}},R,Y,X,Z,U
and
{\underline{a}}
.
These estimates will play a role in the construction of normal form energies in later sections.

3.1.
Sobolev and
BMO
bounds

We begin with the estimates for the auxiliary function
Y:=\frac{{\mathbf{W}}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}
.
Applying Moser type estimates (
2.8
) and (
2.10
), one get

Lemma 3.1

(
[
4
]
)
.

The function
Y
satisfies the BMO bound

(3.1)

\||D|^{\frac{1}{4}}Y\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}A_{\frac{1}{4}},

as well as the Sobolev bounds

\|Y\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}}\lesssim_{A}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{H}^{\sigma}},\quad\||D|^{\sigma}Y\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{W}^{\sigma,4}},\qquad\sigma\geq 0.

We continue with the bounds for the Taylor coefficient
a
.

Lemma 3.2

(
[
4
]
)
.

The Taylor coefficient
a
is nonnegative and satisfies the
BMO
bound and the uniform bound

\|a\|_{BMO}\lesssim\|R\|^{2}_{BMO^{\frac{1}{2}}},\qquad\|a\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim\|R\|^{2}_{\dot{B}^{\frac{1}{2}}_{\infty,2}}.

In addition, it satisfies

\||D|^{\frac{1}{4}}a\|_{BMO}\lesssim AA_{\frac{1}{4}},\quad\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}a\|_{BMO}\lesssim A^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},

and the
L^{4}
based bounds

\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}a\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim A^{\sharp}A_{\frac{1}{4}},\qquad\|a\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim A^{\sharp}A_{-\frac{1}{4}}.

For the
BMO
bound of
a_{1}
, we can rewrite
N
as

N=T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}W+\Pi(W,\bar{R}_{\alpha})-T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}R-\Pi(\bar{{\mathbf{W}}},R)+T_{R_{\alpha}}\bar{W}+\Pi(\bar{W},R_{\alpha})-T_{{\mathbf{W}}}\bar{R}-\Pi({\mathbf{W}},R_{\alpha}).

We use (
2.6
) to estimate the low-high portion of
N
and (
2.3
) to estimate the high-high portion of
N
:

\displaystyle\|N\|_{BMO}\lesssim A_{-\frac{1}{4}}^{2},\quad\gamma\|N\|_{BMO}\lesssim\gamma AA_{-\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim{\underline{A}}^{2},\quad\||D|^{\frac{1}{4}}N\|_{BMO}\lesssim AA_{-\frac{1}{4}},

\displaystyle\gamma\||D|^{\frac{1}{4}}N\|_{BMO}\lesssim\gamma AA_{-\frac{1}{4}}\lesssim A{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

For the
L^{\infty}
bound of
a_{1}
, it is shown in the Proposition
A.4
of
[
17
]
that

\|a_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}A_{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+A).

As for the
L^{4}
based bound of
N
, we write
N
as

N=[\bar{{\mathbf{P}}},\bar{W}]R_{\alpha}-[\bar{{\mathbf{P}}},\bar{R}]{\mathbf{W}}+[{\mathbf{P}},W]\bar{R}_{\alpha}-[{\mathbf{P}},R]\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}.

Using Lemma
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}[\bar{{\mathbf{P}}},\bar{W}]R_{\alpha}\|_{L^{4}}+\gamma\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}[{\mathbf{P}},W]\bar{R}_{\alpha}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\gamma\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}W\|_{BMO}\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}R\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim A^{\sharp}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}},

\displaystyle\gamma\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}[\bar{{\mathbf{P}}},\bar{R}]{\mathbf{W}}\|_{L^{4}}+\gamma\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}[{\mathbf{P}},R]\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim\gamma\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}R\|_{BMO}\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}W\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}},

and the same estimates hold for complex conjugates.
As a consequence,

\gamma\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}N\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}},\quad\gamma\|N\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim A^{\sharp}A_{-\frac{3}{4}}.

Combining the estimates above, we get:

Lemma 3.3
.

The frequency-shift
{\underline{a}}
satisfies the
BMO(L^{\infty})
bounds

(3.2)

\||D|^{\frac{1}{4}}{\underline{a}}\|_{BMO}\lesssim{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}(1+A),\quad\|{\underline{a}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}{\underline{A}}(1+A),

and the
L^{4}
based estimates

(3.3)

\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}{\underline{a}}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim({\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}){\underline{A}^{\sharp}},\qquad\gamma\|{\underline{a}}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim({\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}+\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}){\underline{A}^{\sharp}}.

Next, we recall the bounds for the transport coefficient
b
.

Lemma 3.4

(
[
4
]
)
.

Let
s>0
, then the transport coefficient
b
satisfies

\||D|^{s}b\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}\||D|^{s}R\|_{BMO},\quad\|b\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{A}\|R\|_{\dot{H}^{s}},\quad\||D|^{s}b\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim_{A}\||D|^{s}R\|_{L^{4}}.

In particular, we have

\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}b\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}A,\quad\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}b\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}A_{\frac{1}{4}},\quad\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}b\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim_{A}A^{\sharp}.

For the bound of
b_{1}
, we rewrite
b_{1}
as

\displaystyle b_{1}

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}[W(1-\bar{Y})]-\bar{P}[\bar{W}(1-Y)]=W-\bar{W}+\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[\bar{W}Y]-{\mathbf{P}}[W\bar{Y}]

\displaystyle=W-\bar{W}+T_{Y}\bar{W}+\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}\Pi(\bar{W},Y)-T_{\bar{Y}}W-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(W,\bar{Y}).

Again we use (
2.3
) and (
2.6
) to estimate for
s>0
,

\||D|^{s}b_{1}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}\||D|^{s}W\|_{BMO},\qquad\|b_{1}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}\lesssim_{A}\|W\|_{\dot{H}^{s}},\quad\|b_{1}\|_{\dot{W}^{s,4}}\lesssim_{A}\|W\|_{\dot{W}^{s,4}}.

Gathering together the bounds for
b
and
b_{1}
, we obtain the following result for
b_{1}
:

Lemma 3.5
.

The advection velocity
{\underline{b}}
satisfies the estimates

(3.4)

\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}{\underline{b}}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}{\underline{A}},\quad\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}{\underline{b}}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}},\quad\||D|^{\frac{3}{4}}{\underline{b}}\|_{L^{4}}\lesssim_{A}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}.

As for the auxiliary functions
M
and
M_{1}
, we have

Lemma 3.6

(
[
4
,
17
]
)
.

(1)

The function
M
satisfies the
L^{\infty}
bound

(3.5)

\|M\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}A^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},

as well as the Sobolev bounds

\|M\|_{\dot{H}^{s-\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{A}A\|({\mathbf{W}},R)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}},\quad s\geq 0.

(2)

The auxiliary function
M_{1}
satisfies the
L^{\infty}
bound

(3.6)

\|M_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}A^{2}.

Combining the estimates (
3.5
) and (
3.6
), we immediately get the
L^{\infty}
bound for
\underline{M}

(3.7)

\|\underline{M}\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{A}{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}^{2}.

3.2.
Leading terms of para-material derivatives

The material derivative
D_{t}=\partial_{t}+{\underline{b}}\partial_{\alpha}
is very important in the water waves system.
At the paradifferential level it is replaced by the para-material derivative
T_{D_{t}}=\partial_{t}+T_{\underline{b}}\partial_{\alpha}
.
In this subsection, we compute the leading term of para-material derivatives of various functions.

Lemma 3.7
.

We have the following results on para-material derivatives of
W,{\mathbf{W}}
and
R
:

(1)

Para-material derivative of
W
:

(3.8)

T_{D_{t}}W=-T_{1+W_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})R]+i\frac{\gamma}{2}T_{1+W_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{P}}[W(1-\bar{Y})]-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(W_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})-i\frac{\gamma}{2}W.

(2)

Para-material derivatives of
({\mathbf{W}},R)
:

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}=-T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{W}}-i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{W}}-{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\left[T_{1+W_{\alpha}}[(1-\bar{Y})(R-i\frac{\gamma}{2}W)]+\Pi({\mathbf{W}},{\underline{b}})\right],

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}R=-i\gamma R-{\mathbf{P}}T_{R_{\alpha}}{\underline{b}}-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})+i{\mathbf{P}}[(g+{\underline{a}})Y]-{\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{R}_{\alpha}]-i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}[W\bar{R}_{\alpha}-\bar{W}_{\alpha}R].

(3)

Leading terms of para-material derivatives of
(\mathbf{W},R)
:

(3.9)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}+T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}=G&\\
T_{D_{t}}R-iT_{g+{\underline{a}}}Y+i\gamma R=K,&\end{array}\right.

where the source terms
(G,K)
in (
3.9
) satisfy the BMO bound

\|G\|_{BMO}+\|K\|_{BMO^{\frac{1}{2}}}\lesssim_{\underline{A}}{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},

and the Sobolev bound

\|G\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}+\gamma\|K\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}\lesssim_{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}.

Proof.

For the para-material derivative of
W
, we use the first equation of (
1.6
) to write

T_{D_{t}}W=-T_{1+W_{\alpha}}{\underline{b}}-\Pi(W_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})-i\frac{\gamma}{2}W+\bar{R}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}\bar{W}.

After plugging in the expression of
{\underline{b}}
and applying the Littlewood-Paley projection
{\mathbf{P}}
, we can eliminate the anti-holomorphic portion and obtain the result in
(1)
.
Note that in
T_{D_{t}}
, the advection velocity
{\underline{b}}
has relative low frequency, so that the Littlewood-Paley projection
{\mathbf{P}}
freely passes over it without causing any trouble.

By differentiating (
3.8
), we obtain the para-material derivative of
{\mathbf{W}}
.
Using (
1.9
), we have

T_{D_{t}}R=-T_{R_{\alpha}}{\underline{b}}-\Pi(R_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})+i(g+{\underline{a}})Y-i{\underline{a}}-i\frac{\gamma}{2}(R-\bar{R}).

Applying the projection
{\mathbf{P}}
and using the definition of
{\underline{a}}
, we obtain the para-material derivative of
R
.

For the leading term of
T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}
, all terms in the expression of
T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}
have a good balance of derivatives, with a derivative falling on a low frequency variable, except when the derivative
\partial_{\alpha}
falls on
R
.
Hence, we can rewrite

T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{W}}=-T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}+G,

where the source term
G
is given by

\displaystyle G=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}\Pi(\bar{Y},R_{\alpha})-T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}W_{\alpha}-{\mathbf{P}}T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}[(1-\bar{Y})R]+{\mathbf{P}}T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}[\bar{Y}_{\alpha}R]+{\mathbf{P}}\partial_{\alpha}\Pi({\mathbf{W}},{\underline{b}})

\displaystyle+i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}T_{{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha}}[(1-\bar{Y})W]-i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}[\bar{Y}_{\alpha}W]+i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}T_{\mathbf{W}}{\mathbf{W}}-i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}[\bar{Y}{\mathbf{W}}].

Each term in
G
has a good balanced of derivatives, and satisfies the desired estimate.
The computation of leading term of
T_{D_{t}}R
in part
(3)
is straightforward.
We use the estimates (
3.2
), (
3.3
), (
3.4
) and apply inequalities (
2.1
), (
2.2
), (
2.3
) and (
2.6
) to get the result.
∎

Next, we compute the para-material derivative of
Y:=\frac{{\mathbf{W}}}{1+{\mathbf{W}}}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

The leading term of the para-material derivative of
Y
is given by

(3.10)

T_{D_{t}}Y=-T_{|1-Y|^{2}}R_{\alpha}+G.

The source term
G
satisfies the
BMO
bound

\|G\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

We also have

\||D|^{-\frac{1}{4}}T_{|1-Y|^{2}}R_{\alpha}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}{\underline{A}}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

Proof.

We expand and rewrite the
Y
equation of (
1.9
).

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}Y+T_{|1-Y|^{2}}R_{\alpha}=

\displaystyle-T_{R_{\alpha}}|1-Y|^{2}-\Pi(R_{\alpha},|1-Y|^{2})-T_{Y_{\alpha}}{\underline{b}}-\Pi(Y_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})

\displaystyle+(1-Y)\underline{M}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}\left[Y^{2}+\frac{\bar{Y}}{1-\bar{Y}}Y(1-Y)\right].

The first four terms on the right-hand side have a good balance of derivatives, and they satisfy the
BMO
bound using (
2.3
), (
2.6
) and (
3.1
) inequalities.
For the last two terms, we use (
3.6
) to get the bound.
Hence the right-hand side can be put into the source term
G
.
As for the estimate of the
T_{|1-Y|^{2}}R_{\alpha}
, applying (
2.6
) yields the estimate.
∎

We continue to compute the para-material derivatives of the auxiliary functions
X=T_{1-Y}W
,
Z=T_{1-Y}Q
and
U=T_{1-Y}\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}W
.

Lemma 3.9
.

We have the following results on the para-material derivative of

X
:

(1)

Para-material derivative of
X
:

T_{D_{t}}X=T_{T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}X-{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})R]+i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})W]-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(X_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})-i\frac{\gamma}{2}X+E_{1},

where for
s+\frac{3}{4}\geq 0
, the error
E_{1}
satisfies

\|E_{1}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{5}{4},4}}+\gamma^{2}\|E_{1}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},\qquad\|E_{1}\|_{\dot{H}^{s+\frac{3}{4}}}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}}}\|{\mathbf{W}}\|_{\dot{H}^{s}}.

(2)

Leading term of the para-material derivative of
X
:

T_{D_{t}}X+T_{1-\bar{Y}}R=E_{2},

where the error
E_{2}
satisfies the
BMO
bound

\||D|E_{2}\|_{BMO}+\gamma^{2}\|E_{2}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},

and the Sobolev bound

\|E_{2}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{5}{4},4}}+\gamma^{2}\|E_{2}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}.

(3)

Leading terms of material and para-material derivatives of
X_{\alpha}
:

T_{D_{t}}X_{\alpha}+T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}=E_{3},\qquad D_{t}X_{\alpha}+T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}=E_{3},

where the error
E_{3}
satisfies

\|E_{3}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},\qquad\|E_{3}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}\lesssim_{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}.

(4)

Paradifferential identities relating
{\mathbf{W}},X_{\alpha}
, and
Y
:

(3.11)

X_{\alpha}=T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}}+E_{4}=T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}Y+E_{4},\qquad Y=T_{(1-Y)^{2}}{\mathbf{W}}+E_{4},

where the error
E_{4}
satisfies

\|E_{4}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{2},4}}\lesssim_{A^{\sharp}}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}},\qquad\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{4}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

Proof.

We apply the para-Leibniz rule Lemma
2.5
to
X
,

T_{D_{t}}X=-T_{D_{t}Y}W+T_{1-Y}T_{D_{t}}W+E_{1}.

For the first term on the right-hand side, we use (
1.9
) to write

T_{D_{t}Y}W=-T_{|1-Y|^{2}R_{\alpha}}W+T_{(1-Y)\underline{M}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}[Y^{2}+\frac{\bar{Y}}{1-\bar{Y}}Y(1-Y)]}W.

Here for the term
-T_{|1-Y|^{2}R_{\alpha}}W
, we use (
2.12
) to separate
X=T_{1-Y}W
, and then (
2.3
), (
2.6
) to peel off perturbative components where
R_{\alpha}
have lower or comparative frequencies than
Y
.
The second term belongs to
E_{1}
after applying the bound (
3.7
) directly.

For the second term
T_{1-Y}T_{D_{t}}W
, we rewrite using (
3.8
),

T_{1-Y}T_{D_{t}}W=T_{1-Y}\left(-T_{1+W_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})R]+i\frac{\gamma}{2}T_{1+W_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{P}}[W(1-\bar{Y})]-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(W_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})-i\frac{\gamma}{2}W\right).

After applying (
2.12
), (
2.13
), (
2.14
), (
2.15
) on the right-hand side to distribute and simplify the para-coefficient
T_{1-Y}
, we obtain the para-material derivative of
X
.

For the leading term of
D_{t}X
, we notice that
T_{T_{1-\bar{Y}}R_{\alpha}}X
and
{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(X_{\alpha},{\underline{b}})
have a good balance of derivatives and may be absorbed into
E_{2}
.
The error
E_{1}
is part of
E_{2}
due to the embedding
\dot{W}^{\frac{5}{4},4}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow BMO^{1}(\mathbb{R})
.
For the other terms, we peel off balanced components to write

-{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})R]+i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}[(1-\bar{Y})W]-i\frac{\gamma}{2}X=-T_{1-\bar{Y}}R+i\frac{\gamma}{2}T_{1-\bar{Y}}W+E_{2}.

The remaining two vorticity terms can be combined togther

i\frac{\gamma}{2}T_{1-\bar{Y}}W-i\frac{\gamma}{2}X=i\frac{\gamma}{2}T_{Y-\bar{Y}}W=-\gamma T_{\Im Y}W,

which again may be absorbed into
E_{2}
.

(3)
is a direct consequence of
(1)
and
(2)
, and
(4)
is proved in Lemma
3.2
of
[
4
]
.
∎

Lemma 3.10
.

We have the following results for para-material derivatives of
Z
and
U
:

(1)

Leading term of the para-material derivative of
Z
:

T_{D_{t}}Z-igX+i\gamma Z=E_{1},

where the error
E_{1}
satisfies

\gamma^{2}\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{1}\|_{BMO}+\gamma^{3}\|E_{1}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

(2)

Leading term of the para-material derivative of
U
:

T_{D_{t}}U+T_{1-\bar{Y}}Z=E_{2},

where the error
E_{2}
satisfies

\gamma^{2}\||D|E_{2}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

(3)

We have the relations:

(3.12)

\displaystyle Z_{\alpha}=R+E_{3},

(3.13)

\displaystyle U_{\alpha}=X+E_{4},

where
E_{3}
and
E_{4}
satisfy

\gamma^{3}\|E_{3}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},\quad\gamma\||D|E_{4}\|_{BMO}+\gamma^{3}\|E_{4}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{A}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

(4)

Leading terms of the para-material derivatives of
Z_{\alpha}
and
U_{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}Z_{\alpha}-igT_{1-Y}W_{\alpha}+i\gamma R=E_{5},

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}U_{\alpha}+T_{1-\bar{Y}}R=E_{6},

where the errors
E_{5}
and
E_{6}
satisfy

\displaystyle\gamma\|E_{5}\|_{BMO}+\||D|^{\frac{1}{2}}E_{5}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},

\displaystyle\gamma^{2}\|E_{6}\|_{BMO}+\||D|E_{6}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

Proof.

(1)

We apply the para-Leibniz rule Lemma
2.5
to
Z
,

T_{D_{t}}Z=-T_{D_{t}Y}Q+T_{1-Y}T_{D_{t}}Q+E_{1},

where for
E_{1}
, we have

\gamma^{2}\|E_{1}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{3}{4},4}}+\gamma^{3}\|E_{1}\|_{\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}}\lesssim_{A^{\sharp}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},

so that it satisfies the above error estimate due to the embedding
\dot{W}^{\frac{1}{4},4}(\mathbb{R})\hookrightarrow BMO(\mathbb{R})
.
The term
T_{D_{t}Y}Q
is perturbative, similar to the estimate in Lemma
3.9
.
For
T_{D_{t}}Q
, we use the second equation of (
1.6
) and apply the porjection
{\mathbf{P}}
to write

T_{D_{t}}Q=igW-i\gamma Q+i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}W]-T_{Q_{\alpha}}{\mathbf{P}}{\underline{b}}-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(Q_{\alpha},{\underline{b}}).

Applying
T_{1-Y}
to
T_{D_{t}}Q
, using (
2.13
), (
2.15
) and the fact that
R=(1-Y)Q_{\alpha}
, we have

T_{1-Y}T_{D_{t}}Q=igX-i\gamma Z+i\frac{\gamma}{2}{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}X]-T_{R}{\mathbf{P}}{\underline{b}}-{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R,{\underline{b}})+E_{1}.

The last three terms of the right-hand side may be absorbed into
E
using (
2.3
) and (
2.6
).

(2)

We apply the para-Leibniz rule Lemma
2.5
to
U
,

T_{D_{t}}U=-T_{D_{t}Y}\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}W+T_{1-Y}T_{D_{t}}\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}W+E_{2}.

The first term on the right
T_{D_{t}Y}\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}W
is perturbative, similar to the estimate in Lemma
3.9
.
Using the computation in Lemma
3.9
, one can write

T_{D_{t}}W+T_{1-\bar{Y}}Q_{\alpha}=E_{7},

where the error
E_{7}
satisfies

\gamma^{2}\|E_{7}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

Applying the anti-derivative
\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}
to
T_{D_{t}}W
, one can rewrite using the commutator

T_{D_{t}}\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}W+T_{1-\bar{Y}}Q=[T_{D_{t}},\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}]W-[T_{\bar{Y}},\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}]Q_{\alpha}+E_{2}.

For the two commutators, they are

\displaystyle[T_{D_{t}},\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}]W

\displaystyle=T_{{\underline{b}}}W-\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}(T_{{\underline{b}}}W_{\alpha})=\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}(T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}W),

\displaystyle[T_{\bar{Y}},\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}]Q_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=T_{\bar{Y}}Q-\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}(T_{\bar{Y}}Q_{\alpha})=\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}(T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}Q),

so that they may be absorbed into the error
E_{2}
using (
2.6
).
Applying
T_{1-Y}
to
T_{D_{t}}\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}W
, we obtain the leading term of
T_{D_{t}}U
.

(3)

Taking the derivative of
Z
, we have

Z_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}T_{1-Y}Q=T_{1-Y}Q_{\alpha}-T_{Y_{\alpha}}Q=R+T_{Q_{\alpha}}Y+\Pi(Y,Q_{\alpha})-T_{Y_{\alpha}}Q.

Then the last three terms on the right-hand side go to the error
E_{3}
.

Again applying derivative to
U
, we have

U_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}T_{1-Y}\partial_{\alpha}^{-1}W=T_{1-Y}W-T_{Y_{\alpha}}\partial_{\alpha}^{-1}W=X+E_{4}.

(4)

Taking the derivative of
T_{D_{t}}Z
,

T_{D_{t}}Z_{\alpha}+T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}X_{\alpha}-igX_{\alpha}+i\gamma Z_{\alpha}=E_{5}.

The term
T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}X_{\alpha}
can be absorbed into
E_{5}
.
We change
X_{\alpha}
to
T_{1-Y}{\mathbf{W}}
by (
3.11
), and change
U_{\alpha}
to
X
by (
3.13
).

Similarly, taking the derivative of
T_{D_{t}}U
, we obtain

T_{D_{t}}U_{\alpha}+T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}U_{\alpha}-T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}Z+T_{1-\bar{Y}}Z_{\alpha}=E_{6}.

The terms
T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}U_{\alpha}
and
T_{\bar{Y}_{\alpha}}Z
can be moved into
E_{6}
and we replace
Z_{\alpha}
by
R
using (
3.12
).

∎

Lastly, we compute the material and para-material derivatives of the frequency-shift
{\underline{a}}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The leading terms of the material and para-material derivatives of
{\underline{a}}
are given by

(3.14)

\displaystyle D_{t}{\underline{a}}

\displaystyle=-(g+{\underline{a}})M+\gamma^{2}\Im R-g\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}+E,

(3.15)

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}{\underline{a}}

\displaystyle=-T_{g+{\underline{a}}}M+\gamma^{2}\Im R-g\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}+E,

where the error term
E
satisfies the estimate

(3.16)

\|E\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{\underline{A}}{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

Proof.

First, the leading terms of material derivative (
3.14
) is a direct consequence of the leading terms of the para-material derivative (
3.15
) due to the estimate (
3.2
), (
3.4
) and (
3.5
):

\|\Pi({\underline{b}},{\underline{a}}_{\alpha})\|_{BMO}+\|T_{{\underline{a}}_{\alpha}}{\underline{b}}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{\underline{A}}{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}},\quad\|\Pi(M,{\underline{a}})\|_{BMO}+\|T_{M}{\underline{a}}\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{\underline{A}}{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

Therefore, it only suffices to compute the leading term of the para-material derivative of
{\underline{a}}
(
3.15
).

Next, we compute the para-material derivative of
a
.
Using the para-associativity (
2.16
), the para-Leibniz estimates (
2.17
) (
2.19
) and also (
3.9
), we compute

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{P}}(\bar{R}_{\alpha}R)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}[\partial_{\alpha}T_{D_{t}}\bar{R}\cdot R]+{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}_{\alpha}T_{D_{t}}R]+E

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}[\partial_{\alpha}(-iT_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{Y}+i\gamma\bar{R})\cdot R]+{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}_{\alpha}\cdot(iT_{g+{\underline{a}}}Y-i\gamma R)]+E,

\displaystyle=iT_{g+{\underline{a}}}{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y-\bar{Y}_{\alpha}R]+E

with error
E
satisfying (
3.16
).
Recall the definition of
M
,

M={\mathbf{P}}[R\bar{Y}_{\alpha}-\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y]+\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[\bar{R}Y_{\alpha}-R_{\alpha}\bar{Y}],

we have that

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}a

\displaystyle=-i(T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{P}}(\bar{R}_{\alpha}R)-T_{D_{t}}\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}(R_{\alpha}\bar{R}))

\displaystyle=T_{g+{\underline{a}}}({\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}_{\alpha}Y-\bar{Y}_{\alpha}R]+\bar{{\mathbf{P}}}[R_{\alpha}\bar{Y}-\bar{R}Y_{\alpha}])+E

\displaystyle=T_{g+{\underline{a}}}M+E,

with error
E
satisfying (
3.16
).

We continue to compute the para-material derivatives of the vorticity terms,

\frac{\gamma}{2}T_{D_{t}}(R+\bar{R})=\frac{\gamma}{2}(iT_{g+{\underline{a}}}Y-iT_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{Y}-i\gamma R+i\gamma\bar{R})+E=\gamma^{2}\Im R-g\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}+E.

Finally, we show that
\frac{\gamma}{2}T_{D_{t}}N
can be put into the error
E
.
We compute

\displaystyle T_{D_{t}}{\mathbf{P}}[W\bar{R}_{\alpha}-\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}R]={\mathbf{P}}[T_{D_{t}}W\bar{R}_{\alpha}+WT_{D_{t}}\bar{R}_{\alpha}-T_{D_{t}}\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}R-\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}T_{D_{t}}R]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}[-T_{1+{\mathbf{W}}}T_{1-\bar{Y}}R\cdot\bar{R}_{\alpha}-iT_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{Y}_{\alpha}\cdot W+i\gamma\bar{R}_{\alpha}W+T_{1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}T_{1-Y}\bar{R}_{\alpha}\cdot R-i\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}Y+i\gamma R\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}].

Each term on the right-hand side can be placed into the error term
E
.
For instance, we can rewrite

{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}_{\alpha}W+R\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}]={\mathbf{P}}T_{\bar{R}_{\alpha}}W+{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(\bar{R}_{\alpha},W)+{\mathbf{P}}T_{\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}R+{\mathbf{P}}\Pi(R,\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}),

so that by using (
2.3
) and (
2.6
),

\gamma^{2}\|{\mathbf{P}}[\bar{R}_{\alpha}W+R\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}]\|_{BMO}\lesssim_{\underline{A}}\gamma^{2}A_{-\frac{3}{4}}A_{\frac{1}{4}}\lesssim{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}.

Combining the estimates at each step, we obtain the para-material derivative of
{\underline{a}}
(
3.15
).
∎

4.
Estimates for the linearized equations

In this section, we derive the balanced energy estimates for the linearized system.
Let the solutions for the linearized
water waves around a solution
(W,Q)
to the system (
1.1
) by
(w,q)
and
r:=q-Rw
.
Then it is computed in Section
3
of
[
17
]
that the linearized variables
(w,r)
solve the system

(4.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}(\partial_{t}+\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{b}}\partial_{\alpha})w+\mathbf{P}\left[\dfrac{1}{1+\bar{\mathbf{W}}}r_{\alpha}\right]+\mathbf{P}\left[\dfrac{R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{\mathbf{W}}}w\right]+\gamma\mathbf{P}\left[\dfrac{\Im{\mathbf{W}}}{1+\bar{\mathbf{W}}}w\right]=\mathbf{P}\underline{\mathcal{G}_{0}}(w,r)&\\
(\partial_{t}+\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{b}}\partial_{\alpha})r+i\gamma r-i\mathbf{P}\left[\dfrac{g+\underline{a}}{1+\mathbf{W}}w\right]=\mathbf{P}\underline{\mathcal{K}_{0}}(w,r),&\end{array}\right.

where
\mathfrak{M}_{\underline{b}}f={\mathbf{P}}[{\underline{b}}f]
,
and the source terms
\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{0}(w,r),\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{0}(w,r)
are given by

(4.2)

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{0}(w,r)=\mathcal{G}(w,r)-i\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathcal{G}_{1}(w,r),\quad\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{0}(w,r)=\mathcal{K}(w,r)-i\frac{\gamma}{2}\mathcal{K}_{1}(w,r),

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(w,r)=(1+\mathbf{W})(\mathbf{P}\bar{m}+\bar{\mathbf{P}}m),\quad\mathcal{G}_{1}(w,r)=-(1+\mathbf{W})(\mathbf{P}\bar{m}_{1}-\bar{\mathbf{P}}m_{1}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(w,r)=\bar{\mathbf{P}}n-\mathbf{P}\bar{n},\quad\mathcal{K}_{1}(w,r)=\bar{\mathbf{P}}m_{2}+\mathbf{P}\bar{m}_{2},\quad n:=\frac{\bar{R}(r_{\alpha}+R_{\alpha}w)}{1+\mathbf{W}},

as well as

\displaystyle m:=\frac{q_{\alpha}-Rw_{\alpha}}{J}+\frac{\bar{R}w_{\alpha}}{(1+\mathbf{W})^{2}}=\frac{r_{\alpha}+R_{\alpha}w}{J}+\frac{\bar{R}w_{\alpha}}{(1+\mathbf{W})^{2}},

\displaystyle m_{1}:=\frac{1}{1+\bar{\mathbf{W}}}w-\frac{\bar{W}}{(1+{\mathbf{W}})^{2}}w_{\alpha},\quad m_{2}:=\bar{R}w-\frac{\bar{W}r_{\alpha}+\bar{W}R_{\alpha}w}{1+\mathbf{W}}.

We define the associated linear energy

E^{(2)}_{lin}(w,r)=\int(g+\underline{a})|w|^{2}+\Im(r\bar{r}_{\alpha})\,d\alpha.

Then it is shown in
[
17
]
the following quadratic energy estimate for large data:

Proposition 4.1

(
[
17
]
)
.

The linearized system (
4.1
) is locally well-posed in
L^{2}\times\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and the following properties hold:

(i)

Norm equivalence:

E^{(2)}_{lin}(w,r)\approx_{\underline{A}}\mathcal{E}_{0}(w,r).

(ii)

Energy estimate:

\frac{d}{dt}E^{(2)}_{lin}(w,r)\lesssim_{A}({\underline{B}}+\gamma{\underline{A}})E^{(2)}_{lin}(w,r).

In this section, we prove the following energy estimate for small data of (
4.1
).

Theorem 4.2
.

Assume that
\max\{{\underline{A}},{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}\}\lesssim 1
.
Then the linearized system (
4.1
) is locally well-posed in
\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}
.
Moreover, there exists an energy functional
E^{\frac{1}{4}}_{lin}(w,r)
with the following properties:

(1)

Norm equivalence:

E^{\frac{1}{4}}_{lin}(w,r)\approx_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}\|(w,r)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}}+O(\gamma^{4}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}^{2})\|(w,r)\|^{2}_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{-\frac{3}{4}}}.

(2)

Energy estimate:

\frac{d}{dt}E^{\frac{1}{4}}_{lin}(w,r)\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}+{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}})E^{\frac{1}{4}}_{lin}(w,r).

Compared to the previous result Proposition
4.1
, in our theorem, the coefficient of the energy estimate does not depend on the pointwise control norm
{\underline{B}}
.
It merely depends on
{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}
, which can be seen as an intermediate control norm between
{\underline{A}}
and
{\underline{B}}
, and also the
L^{4}
based control norm
{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}
.

\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}
is the minimal Sobolev regularity one can expect for
(w,r)
to have the above balanced energy estimate.

From the linearized equations (
4.1
) we obtain the corresponding paradifferential flow

(4.3)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}T_{D_{t}}w+T_{1-\bar{Y}}\partial_{\alpha}r+T_{(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}}w+\gamma T_{\Im{\mathbf{W}}}w=0&\\
T_{D_{t}}r+i\gamma r-iT_{1-Y}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}w=0.&\end{array}\right.

In the following, we will fix a self-adjoint quantization for
T
.
To achieve this, we may use the Weyl quantization, or simply the average
\frac{1}{2}(T+T^{*})
.
Using the self-adjoint quantization, later for computations of the integrals such as (
4.7
), for any real-valued function
f
, one can distribute the para-coefficient
T_{f}
so that

\int T_{f}g\cdot h\,d\alpha=\int g\cdot T_{f}h\,d\alpha,\quad\forall f,g\in L^{2}.

This will make our computation easier by avoiding the estimates for
(T_{f})^{*}
.

The linearized equations (
4.1
) can be rewritten in the paradifferential form

(4.4)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}T_{D_{t}}w+T_{1-\bar{Y}}\partial_{\alpha}r+T_{(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}}w+\gamma T_{\Im{\mathbf{W}}}w=\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}(w,r)&\\
T_{D_{t}}r+i\gamma r-iT_{1-Y}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}w=\mathcal{K}^{\sharp}(w,r),&\end{array}\right.

where the source terms
(\mathcal{G}^{\sharp},\mathcal{K}^{\sharp})
are given by

\mathcal{G}^{\sharp}={\mathbf{P}}(\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{0}+\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{1}),\qquad\mathcal{K}^{\sharp}={\mathbf{P}}(\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{0}+\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{1}),

with
(\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{0},\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{0})
are as (
4.2
) and

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{1}=

\displaystyle(T_{r_{\alpha}}\bar{Y}+\Pi(r_{\alpha},\bar{Y}))-(T_{w_{\alpha}}{\underline{b}}+\Pi(w_{\alpha},{\underline{b}}))-(T_{w}((1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}})

\displaystyle+\Pi(w,(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}})),

\displaystyle\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=

\displaystyle-(T_{r_{\alpha}}{\underline{b}}+\Pi(r_{\alpha},{\underline{b}}))+i(T_{1-Y}T_{w}{\underline{a}}+T_{1-Y}\Pi(w,{\underline{a}})-T_{(g+{\underline{a}})w}Y-\Pi((g+{\underline{a}})w,Y))

are the paradifferential truncations.

The proof of Theorem
4.2
is divided into the following steps.
First, we consider a variant of (
4.4
) with more general right-hand side
(G,K)
,

(4.5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}T_{D_{t}}w+T_{1-\bar{Y}}\partial_{\alpha}r+T_{(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}}w+\gamma T_{\Im{\mathbf{W}}}w=G&\\
T_{D_{t}}r+i\gamma r-iT_{1-Y}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}w=K.&\end{array}\right.

Under this setting, one can prove the following result.

Proposition 4.3
.

Assume that
\max\{{\underline{A}},{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}\}
is small, then the homogeneous paradifferential system (
4.3
) is locally well-posed in
\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}
for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
.
Furthermore, for each
s
, there exists an energy functional
E^{s,para}_{lin}(w,r)
such that we have

(1)

The norm equivalence:

E^{s,para}_{lin}(w,r)\approx_{A^{\sharp}}\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}}^{2}.

(2)

The time derivative of
E^{s,para}_{lin}(w,r)
is bounded by

\frac{d}{dt}E^{s,para}_{lin}(w,r)\lesssim{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}+{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}})\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}}^{2}.

We will first prove the easier case for
s=0
, and then consider the case for more general
s
.
Clearly, Theorem
4.2
will follow directly from Proposition
4.3
as long as the source terms
(\mathcal{G}^{\sharp},\mathcal{K}^{\sharp})
satisfy for
s=\frac{1}{4}
,

(4.6)

\|(\mathcal{G}^{\sharp},\mathcal{K}^{\sharp})\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}}\lesssim{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}(\gamma^{\frac{1}{2}}+{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}})\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}}.

Next, we take into account the nonlinear source terms
(\mathcal{G}^{\sharp},\mathcal{K}^{\sharp})
on the right-hand side.
Unfortunately, the source terms
(\mathcal{G}^{\sharp},\mathcal{K}^{\sharp})
do not satisfy the bound (
4.6
) for any
s
, because of both the quadratic contributions and unbalanced cubic contributions.

In order to deal with these unfavourable source terms, we will use the paradifferential normal form analysis to construct the modified normal form linear variables
(w_{NF},r_{NF})
.
We work at a specific regularity level, namely
s=\frac{1}{4}
, as this is the minimal Sobolev index that allows us to obtain the perturbative bounds in (
4.6
).
With these new modified normal form linear variables
(w_{NF},r_{NF})
, the source terms become perturbative and satisfy the bound of (
4.6
) type.

Proposition 4.4
.

Assuming that
(w,r)
solve the linearized paradifferential system (
4.4
), then there exist modified normal form linear variables
(w_{NF},r_{NF})
satisfying (
4.5
) and that we have

(1)

Invertibility:

\|(w_{NF},r_{NF})-(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}}}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}\left(\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}}+\gamma^{2}\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{-\frac{3}{4}}}\right).

(2)

Perturbative source terms:

\|(G,K)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}}\lesssim_{{\underline{A}^{\sharp}}}{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}\left(\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{\frac{1}{4}}}+\gamma^{2}\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{-\frac{3}{4}}}\right).

Remark 3
.

It is possible to improve the above source term bound, replacing
{\underline{A}_{\frac{1}{4}}}{\underline{A}^{\sharp}_{\frac{1}{4}}}
by a slightly smaller constant
{\underline{A}}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}
as in
[
4
]
.
However, this not only requires more delicate estimates for the para-material derivatives and more steps of paradifferential normal form corrections, but also does not help in the proof of the local well-posedness.
We will not prove this sharper version of estimates here.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of the above results.
In Section
4.1
, we compute the time derivative for the linear paradifferential energy
E^{0,para}_{lin}
when
s=0
.
Then in Section
4.2
, we consider for general
s\in\mathbb{R}
.

E^{0,para}_{lin}(|D|^{s}w,|D|^{s}r)
does not satisfy our need for paradifferential energy because it brings additional nonperturbative source terms.
In order to eliminate these bad terms, we use the paradifferential conjugation to construct new variables
(\tilde{w}^{s},\tilde{r}^{s})
.
This change of variables reduces the source terms of paradifferential equations to balanced ones, thus proving Proposition
4.3
.
In the rest of Section
4
we take into account the effect of source terms
(\mathcal{G}^{\sharp},\mathcal{K}^{\sharp})
.
In Section
4.3
, we compute the
\mathcal{H}
bound of
(\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{0},\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{0})
and its leading parts.
Then we compute the material and para-material derivatives of
(w,r)
,
x:=T_{1-Y}w
, and
u:=T_{1-Y}\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}w
.
Next, in Section
4.4
, we construct the normal form corrections that remove
(\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{1},\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{1})
up to balanced cubic terms.
Finally in Section
4.5
, we construct the normal form corrections that remove the leading term of
(\underline{\mathcal{G}}_{0},\underline{\mathcal{K}}_{0})
.
After these paradifferential normal form transformations, the system is finally reduced to the desired form, and this finishes the proof of Theorem
4.2
.

4.1.
\mathcal{H}^{0}
bound for the paradifferential equation

In this subsection, we prove Proposition
4.3
in the case
s=0
.
Following the setup in
[
4
]
, we consider the paradifferential energy

E^{0,para}_{lin}(w,r)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}w\cdot\bar{w}+\Im(r\bar{r}_{\alpha})\,d\alpha.

Clearly, we have the norm equivalence since

\|T_{\underline{a}}w\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\|{\underline{a}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|w\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim_{A}O({\underline{A}})\|w\|_{L^{2}}.

The assumption
{\underline{A}}\lesssim 1
ensures that
E^{0,para}_{lin}(w,r)
is a positive energy.
As for the time derivative of the energy, we have the following computation.

Proposition 4.5
.

Suppose that
(w,r)
solve the (
4.5
), and
(G,K)\in L^{2}\times\dot{H}^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then the time derivative of the paradifferential energy is:

(4.7)

\frac{d}{dt}E^{0,para}_{lin}=2\Re\int T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{w}G-i\bar{r}_{\alpha}K\,d\alpha+\gamma\int T_{\gamma\Im R-2g\Im{\mathbf{W}}}\bar{w}\cdot w\,d\alpha+O_{{\underline{A}}}(\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}})E^{0,para}_{lin}.

Proof.

By direct computation, and the self-adjointness of
T
,

\frac{d}{dt}E^{0,para}_{lin}(w,r)=2\Re\int T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{w}\cdot w_{t}\,d\alpha+2\Im\int\bar{r}_{\alpha}r_{t}\,d\alpha+\int T_{{\underline{a}}_{t}}\bar{w}\cdot w\,d\alpha.

The strategy here is to replace the time derivatives
w_{t}
and
r_{t}
by para-material derivatives
T_{D_{t}}w
and
T_{D_{t}}r
, so that we can use the system (
4.5
).
Using integration by parts, we write

\displaystyle 2\Re\int

\displaystyle T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{w}\cdot T_{{\underline{b}}}w_{\alpha}\,d\alpha=-\int T_{((g+{\underline{a}}){\underline{b}})_{\alpha}}\bar{w}\cdot w\,d\alpha

\displaystyle-\int(T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}T_{{\underline{a}}}+T_{{\underline{b}}}T_{{\underline{a}}_{\alpha}}-T_{({\underline{a}}{\underline{b}})_{\alpha}})\bar{w}\cdot w\,d\alpha-\int(T_{\underline{b}}T_{\underline{a}}-T_{\underline{a}}T_{\underline{b}})\bar{w}_{\alpha}\cdot w\,d\alpha,

\displaystyle 2\Im\int

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{\alpha}\cdot T_{\underline{b}}\partial_{\alpha}r\,d\alpha=0.

The above commutator integrals satisfy

\int(T_{{\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}T_{{\underline{a}}}+T_{{\underline{b}}}T_{{\underline{a}}_{\alpha}}-T_{({\underline{a}}{\underline{b}})_{\alpha}})\bar{w}\cdot w\,d\alpha+\int(T_{\underline{b}}T_{\underline{a}}-T_{\underline{a}}T_{\underline{b}})\bar{w}_{\alpha}\cdot w\,d\alpha=O_{\underline{A}}(\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}})\|w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

due to the para-product estimate (
2.12
), the para-commutator estimate (
2.11
) and also the
BMO
bounds (
3.2
) and (
3.4
).

Adding the
T_{\underline{b}}
integral to the energy estimate, and using (
4.5
), we get that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}E^{0,para}_{lin}(w,r)=

\displaystyle 2\Re\int T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{w}\cdot T_{D_{t}}w\,d\alpha+2\Im\int\bar{r}_{\alpha}T_{D_{t}}r\,d\alpha

\displaystyle+\int T_{{\underline{a}}_{t}+{\underline{b}}{\underline{a}}_{\alpha}+(g+{\underline{a}}){\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}\bar{w}\cdot wd\alpha+O_{\underline{A}}(\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}})\|w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Re\int T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{w}\cdot G-i\bar{r}_{\alpha}K-T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{w}\cdot T_{(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}}w\,d\alpha

\displaystyle+\int T_{{\underline{a}}_{t}+{\underline{b}}{\underline{a}}_{\alpha}+(g+{\underline{a}}){\underline{b}}_{\alpha}}\bar{w}\cdot wd\alpha+O_{\underline{A}}(\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}})\|w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\Re\int T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\bar{w}\cdot G-i\bar{r}_{\alpha}K\,d\alpha+O_{\underline{A}}(\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}})\|w\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\int T_{D_{t}{\underline{a}}+(g+{\underline{a}})({\underline{b}}_{\alpha}-2\Re\frac{R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}-2\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}})}\bar{w}\cdot w\,d\alpha.

Here, using (
3.14
) and the definition of
\underline{M}
, we have

\displaystyle D_{t}{\underline{a}}

\displaystyle+(g+{\underline{a}})\left({\underline{b}}_{\alpha}-2\Re\frac{R_{\alpha}}{1+\bar{{\mathbf{W}}}}-2\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}\right)=D_{t}{\underline{a}}-(g+{\underline{a}})(\underline{M}+\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}})

\displaystyle=-2(g+{\underline{a}})M+\gamma^{2}\Im R-2g\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}+i\frac{\gamma}{2}(g+{\underline{a}})M_{1}-{\underline{a}}\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}+O_{\underline{A}}(\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{2}\Im R-2g\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}+O_{\underline{A}}(\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}),

where we use the
L^{\infty}
bounds (
3.2
), (
3.5
), and (
3.6
).
Gathering all the terms, we obtain the estimate (
4.7
).
∎

The local well-posedness of the homogeneous paradifferential flow in
\mathcal{H}
follows by a direct fixed point argument.

4.2.
\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}
bounds for the linear paradifferential flow.

We now consider more general
s\in\mathbb{R}
for Proposition
4.3
, and prove the
\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}
well-posedness of the linear paradifferential flow.
We will construct the following variables
(\tilde{w}^{s},\tilde{r}^{s})
:

Proposition 4.6
.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
, and
(w,r)
solve the linear paradifferential flow (
4.3
).
Then there exist linearized, normalized variables
(\tilde{w}^{s},\tilde{r}^{s})
solving

(4.8)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}T_{D_{t}}\tilde{w}^{s}+T_{1-\bar{Y}}\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{r}^{s}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}}\tilde{w}^{s}=G_{s}&\\
T_{D_{t}}\tilde{r}^{s}+i\gamma\tilde{r}^{s}-iT_{1-Y}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}\tilde{w}^{s}=K_{s},&\end{array}\right.

and such that

\|(\tilde{w}^{s},\tilde{r}^{s})-|D|^{s}(w,r)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim_{\underline{A}}{\underline{A}}\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}},

(4.9)

\|(G_{s},K_{s})\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim_{\underline{A}}\underline{A}^{2}_{\frac{1}{4}}\|(w,r)\|_{\dot{\mathcal{H}}^{s}}.

Then Proposition
4.3
follows by the fixed point argument and choosing

E^{s,para}_{lin}(w,r)=E^{0,para}_{lin}(\tilde{w}^{s},\tilde{r}^{s}).

Proof.

A first idea to this problem is to consider the variables

(\tilde{w}^{s},\tilde{r}^{s}):=(|D|^{s}w,|D|^{s}r).

The new variables
(\tilde{w}^{s},\tilde{r}^{s})
solve the system

(4.10)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lr}T_{D_{t}}w^{s}+T_{1-\bar{Y}}\partial_{\alpha}r^{s}+T_{(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}+\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}}w^{s}=\mathcal{G}^{s}_{0}&\\
T_{D_{t}}r^{s}+i\gamma r^{s}-iT_{1-Y}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}w^{s}=\mathcal{K}^{s}_{0},&\end{array}\right.

where the source terms
(\mathcal{G}^{s}_{0},\mathcal{K}^{s}_{0})
are given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}^{s}_{0}=L({\underline{b}}_{\alpha},w^{s})-L(\bar{Y}_{\alpha},r^{s})+L([(1-\bar{Y})R_{\alpha}]_{\alpha}+\gamma\Im{\mathbf{W}}_{\alpha},\partial^{-1}_{\alpha}w^{s}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}^{s}_{0}=L({\underline{b}}_{\alpha},r^{s})+iL(Y_{\alpha},\partial_{\alpha}^{-1}T_{g+{\underline{a}}}w^{s}).

Here
L
denotes the order zero paradifferential commutator

L(f_{\alpha},u)=-[|D|^{s},T_{f}]\partial_{\alpha}|D|^{-s}u\approx-sT_{f_{\alpha}}u+\text{lower order terms}.

Unfortunately, the source terms
(\mathcal{G}^{s}_{0},\mathcal{K}^{s}_{0})
do not satisfy the bounds (
4.9
), so that they cannot be treated perturbatively.
Even after applying the normal form transformation, there are still some unbalanced quadratic and higher order terms left.

To eliminate these unbalanced terms, we first apply the paradifferential conjugation, which is similar to the renormalization approach in
[
